I wouldn’t say I’m doing wild, reckless things, but I do notice I jump in before I’ve really thought through whether I should.\n\nThe biggest example is conversations. I’ve been cutting people off more than I’d like to admit. Like with my wife—if she’s talking to her parents, I’ll hear part of it and suddenly I’m jumping in with my opinion or answering something that wasn’t even directed at me. And then I can see her kind of give me that look like, “Please let me finish,” or “This isn’t your conversation,” and I feel bad afterward.\n\nIt happens at work too. I’ll be with an employee and they’re explaining an issue, and before they’re done, I’m already finishing their thought or giving the solution I think they’re getting to. In my head, it feels like I’m being efficient, like I already see where it’s going, but I can tell it rubs people the wrong way. They’ll pause or get a little quiet, and then I realize I sort of steamrolled them.\n\nSo yeah, it’s less like I’m making huge impulsive decisions and more that my mouth gets ahead of me. I jump in before I’ve taken a second to think, “Should I actually speak right now, or should I let them finish?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is starting to cause problems, even if I’m not completely falling apart or anything. Like, I’m still getting through my responsibilities overall—I’m not missing everything or unable to function—but it feels like it’s costing me more than it used to.\n\nAt work, especially in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people are getting less patient with me. If I interrupt or move the conversation along too fast, or if I lose the thread and have to circle back, it changes the tone. People still respect me, I think, but there’s this little hesitation sometimes, like they’re bracing for me to jump in or derail things. And that bothers me because I don’t want people feeling like they can’t finish a thought around me.\n\nAt home it’s probably more personal. It’s starting to affect trust a little—not like anyone thinks I’m a bad person, but if we make family plans or my wife expects me to be fully present in something, and then I get sidetracked or push my way into a conversation, it creates frustration. I can see that “here we go again” look sometimes. So, yeah, I’m keeping up on paper, but it’s definitely creating friction with people I care about and people I work with.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is brand new. I mean, I probably notice it more now because I’m an adult and there are more consequences, but when I really think about it, this was around when I was a kid too.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat in class because I talked too much or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to. I’d blurt things out before they even finished asking the question, like I thought I knew where they were going and I just had to answer right then. At the time I think people just saw it as me being disruptive or not having manners, and honestly I probably didn’t think much of it either. But now, hearing myself describe the same kind of thing happening in meetings or conversations, it feels like there’s a line connecting it back to school.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it like every single day of my life has been some dramatic problem, but when I look back, it feels like the same pattern has kind of always been there.\n\nAs a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt out answers before they were even done asking the question. I remember feeling like I already knew what they were going to say, so I’d just jump in, and then I’d get in trouble for interrupting or being disruptive.\n\nAt the time, I don’t think I had any big explanation for it. It was just sort of, “That’s how I was.” But now, looking at how I still jump in or have trouble holding back, it does feel pretty consistent from when I was younger to now.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “significantly” is where I kind of hesitate, because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not functioning at all. I am still keeping up overall. Work is getting done, I’m still showing up for my family, I’m not in some total collapse or anything like that.\n\nBut it is affecting things, and that’s the part that worries me. At work, especially in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people’s patience wears thin. If the conversation doesn’t go the way it should because I jump in too fast, or push it in a different direction, or make it harder for the other person to finish their thought, it changes how people respond to me. It’s not like they’re yelling at me, but there’s this shift, like they’re managing me a little.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more about trust. If we have family plans or my wife is expecting me to be fully present and things get sidetracked, it feels like I’m slowly spending down credibility. Like, I’ll say I’m going to be engaged or follow the plan, and then it doesn’t go smoothly, and I can see the frustration. So I’d say I’m functioning, yes, but it’s starting to cost me in relationships and in how much patience people have with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, I didn’t think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back at elementary school, it was definitely there.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d be getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, like without asking. And I remember blurting things out a lot—like they’d be halfway through a question and I’d already jump in with the answer because I thought I knew where they were going. At the time it was just treated like I was being disruptive or not listening, but now when I think back on it, it does sound like the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, a relationship, or an important opportunity because of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or had a relationship end because of it, no. Not in that clean, obvious way where someone says, “This happened because you can’t sit still or you interrupt too much,” or something like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m still keeping up overall. I’m still working, still doing what I need to do, still present in my family’s life. But I can feel it costing me in smaller ways that add up. At work, if a meeting gets derailed because I jump in too much, or a mentoring conversation doesn’t go the way it should because I’m moving too fast or not letting the other person finish, people don’t necessarily punish me for it, but their patience changes. You can feel when people start managing around you a little.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar but more personal. If we have plans and I’m supposed to be engaged and the whole thing gets sidetracked, it chips away at trust. Not like my marriage is over or anything, but more like my wife has less confidence that things will go smoothly when I say they will. So I haven’t lost some major thing outright, but I do feel like I’m slowly spending down patience and trust, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity have significantly impacted your ability to succeed in your career or maintain healthy relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say yes, but I keep wanting to qualify it because I don’t feel like I’ve completely failed or fallen apart. I’m still keeping up overall. I’m working, I’m handling responsibilities, I’m present at home. So if “significantly impacted” means I can’t function, then no, it’s not that.\n\nBut if it means it’s starting to affect how people experience me and how much confidence they have in me, then yeah. At work, especially in meetings or mentoring conversations, if things don’t go the way they should because I move too fast or the conversation gets pulled off track, I can feel people’s patience thin out. It’s subtle, but it’s there. They might not say anything directly, but the room changes a little.\n\nAnd at home it’s more about trust. If we have family plans or my wife is counting on me to stay engaged and things get sidetracked, it feels like I’m chipping away at reliability. Like I’m not destroying the relationship, but I’m making it harder for her to trust that things will go smoothly when I say they will.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m succeeding on paper, but it’s costing me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on something that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely gotten wrapped up in things before, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as losing awareness of everything around me.\n\nWhen I hear it phrased that way, I think of someone who sits down and then suddenly hours have gone by and they forgot to eat or missed a bunch of important stuff. I don’t know that I have a clear example like that. I can get into something and be pretty absorbed, especially if it’s interesting or urgent, but I’m usually still kind of aware of the clock or people around me.\n\nSo… maybe mildly sometimes, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a major pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so intensely focused on a task or activity that you tuned out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that intense way. I mean, I can get interested in something and spend longer on it than I meant to, but when you say “tuned out everything else,” I picture someone who doesn’t hear people talking to them or completely forgets what’s happening around them, and that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nI’m usually at least partly aware of what’s going on. Like, I might be focused, but I’m not gone, if that makes sense. I don’t have a strong example where I was so locked in that the rest of the world disappeared. So I’d probably say no, or at least not something I’ve noticed as a real pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been noticeably harder to manage for about… a year and two months, maybe around that.\n\nIt really became more obvious after the conflict with my in-laws started taking up more space in our family life. Before that, I’m not saying everything was perfect, but it felt more manageable, like I could kind of compensate or keep things contained. Since then, it’s been harder to stay on top of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it’s not just a few bad weeks or a short stressful patch. It’s been going on long enough that I can’t really write it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in the workplace, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it shows up in more than one place. I’m still functioning, like I’m not dropping everything or failing at work or anything that dramatic, but it’s starting to have a cost.\n\nAt home, it affects trust a bit. If family plans don’t go the way they should, or I don’t handle something the way I said I would, it’s not always seen as just a one-off anymore. It becomes this thing where people are bracing for me to be scattered or impatient or not fully steady with it, and that’s hard to admit.\n\nAnd at work, I can usually keep the bigger picture moving because that’s kind of my role, but in meetings or mentoring conversations, people can get frustrated with me. Not necessarily in a huge blowup way, more like I can feel their patience wearing thin when the conversation doesn’t stay as smooth or productive as it should.\n\nSo yes, home and work for sure. Socially maybe less obvious, but the main places are definitely family and work.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a young child, before the age of 7?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t have perfect memory from before age 7, obviously, but looking back, that pattern was there when I was a kid.\n\nI remember teachers moving my seat around a lot because I talked too much or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to. Not in a trying-to-be-bad way, at least not how I remember it. It was more like I’d just already be doing it before I thought about it. And I’d blurt things out before the teacher finished asking the question, which got me in trouble pretty regularly.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say it goes back pretty early. At the time people just treated it like I was energetic or disruptive, but looking back now, it does feel like the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems in your relationships with friends or family members because of your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly with family. I wouldn’t say my relationships are falling apart or anything like that, but it has definitely created strain.\n\nAt home, if there’s a plan I said I’d handle, or something needs to go a certain way and I’m not really on top of it, it can turn into more than just the one situation. It’s like people start to wonder whether they can count on me to follow through smoothly. And that hurts, because I do care, and I’m not trying to be careless or make things harder for anyone.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m still keeping up overall, but there’s a cost. The trust gets chipped at a little. Family members can get frustrated because they feel like they have to double-check me or brace for me to be impatient or scattered when plans don’t go the way they should.\n\nWith friends, I think it’s less obvious, or maybe I hide it better. But with family, yes, it has caused real tension. And at work too, not family obviously, but in meetings or mentoring conversations I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me when the conversation doesn’t go as smoothly as it should. So I’d say yes, it affects relationships, especially at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’m unable to do my job or take care of daily responsibilities, exactly. That feels too strong. I’m still keeping up overall, and from the outside I probably look like I’m managing most things.

But yes, I do feel like I’m not always performing at the level people expect from me, especially in the way things get handled. At work, I can keep the bigger responsibilities moving, but meetings or mentoring conversations don’t always go as smoothly as they should. Sometimes I can tell people are getting impatient with me, not because the whole thing has collapsed, but because it takes more effort than it should or I’m not as steady in the conversation as they need me to be.

And at home, it’s similar. Family plans or things I said I’d handle can go sideways, and then it starts costing me trust. It’s not like I’m completely failing my responsibilities, but people start bracing themselves a little, like they’re expecting me to drop the ball or make things harder. That part bothers me a lot.

So… yes, it affects my level of performance, but more in this creeping way. I’m still getting by, but it takes a toll on trust at home and patience from people at work.

Like, embarrassingly easily. I’ll be in my office trying to focus on something, and my phone will buzz once—not even something important—and suddenly I’m checking it, then thinking about something else, and by the time I look back I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if someone just walks past my office, even if they’re not talking to me, my attention goes right to them. And sometimes it’s not even something outside—just a random thought, like something about my in-laws or some little thing I need to handle, pops into my head and it yanks me away before I can stop it. It’s like the smallest thing can interrupt me, and I’m sitting there thinking, “Why did I just lose ten minutes to that?” That’s really what pushed me to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not every single second, but it happens a lot when I’m supposed to just sit still and be present. Like at family dinners, for example, I’ll be sitting there and everyone’s talking, and I’m trying to be normal and just sit, but inside it feels like there’s this little motor running. I’ll start bouncing my leg or shifting in the chair, or I need to mess with a napkin, a fork, my ring—anything in my hands.\n\nSame kind of thing happens in church volunteer meetings. I know I’m supposed to just listen, and I don’t want to be rude, but I get this feeling like I need to move or do something. It’s not even that I’m anxious exactly, it’s more like my body just doesn’t want to stay parked. I can sit there, but it takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m doing wild, reckless things, but I do notice I jump in before I’ve really thought through whether I should.\n\nThe biggest example is conversations. I’ve been cutting people off more than I’d like to admit. Like with my wife—if she’s talking to her parents, I’ll hear part of it and suddenly I’m jumping in with my opinion or answering something that wasn’t even directed at me. And then I can see her kind of give me that look like, “Please let me finish,” or “This isn’t your conversation,” and I feel bad afterward.\n\nIt happens at work too. I’ll be with an employee and they’re explaining an issue, and before they’re done, I’m already finishing their thought or giving the solution I think they’re getting to. In my head, it feels like I’m being efficient, like I already see where it’s going, but I can tell it rubs people the wrong way. They’ll pause or get a little quiet, and then I realize I sort of steamrolled them.\n\nSo yeah, it’s less like I’m making huge impulsive decisions and more that my mouth gets ahead of me. I jump in before I’ve taken a second to think, “Should I actually speak right now, or should I let them finish?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is starting to cause problems, even if I’m not completely falling apart or anything. Like, I’m still getting through my responsibilities overall—I’m not missing everything or unable to function—but it feels like it’s costing me more than it used to.\n\nAt work, especially in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people are getting less patient with me. If I interrupt or move the conversation along too fast, or if I lose the thread and have to circle back, it changes the tone. People still respect me, I think, but there’s this little hesitation sometimes, like they’re bracing for me to jump in or derail things. And that bothers me because I don’t want people feeling like they can’t finish a thought around me.\n\nAt home it’s probably more personal. It’s starting to affect trust a little—not like anyone thinks I’m a bad person, but if we make family plans or my wife expects me to be fully present in something, and then I get sidetracked or push my way into a conversation, it creates frustration. I can see that “here we go again” look sometimes. So, yeah, I’m keeping up on paper, but it’s definitely creating friction with people I care about and people I work with.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is brand new. I mean, I probably notice it more now because I’m an adult and there are more consequences, but when I really think about it, this was around when I was a kid too.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat in class because I talked too much or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to. I’d blurt things out before they even finished asking the question, like I thought I knew where they were going and I just had to answer right then. At the time I think people just saw it as me being disruptive or not having manners, and honestly I probably didn’t think much of it either. But now, hearing myself describe the same kind of thing happening in meetings or conversations, it feels like there’s a line connecting it back to school.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it like every single day of my life has been some dramatic problem, but when I look back, it feels like the same pattern has kind of always been there.\n\nAs a kid, teachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt out answers before they were even done asking the question. I remember feeling like I already knew what they were going to say, so I’d just jump in, and then I’d get in trouble for interrupting or being disruptive.\n\nAt the time, I don’t think I had any big explanation for it. It was just sort of, “That’s how I was.” But now, looking at how I still jump in or have trouble holding back, it does feel pretty consistent from when I was younger to now.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “significantly” is where I kind of hesitate, because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m not functioning at all. I am still keeping up overall. Work is getting done, I’m still showing up for my family, I’m not in some total collapse or anything like that.\n\nBut it is affecting things, and that’s the part that worries me. At work, especially in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people’s patience wears thin. If the conversation doesn’t go the way it should because I jump in too fast, or push it in a different direction, or make it harder for the other person to finish their thought, it changes how people respond to me. It’s not like they’re yelling at me, but there’s this shift, like they’re managing me a little.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more about trust. If we have family plans or my wife is expecting me to be fully present and things get sidetracked, it feels like I’m slowly spending down credibility. Like, I’ll say I’m going to be engaged or follow the plan, and then it doesn’t go smoothly, and I can see the frustration. So I’d say I’m functioning, yes, but it’s starting to cost me in relationships and in how much patience people have with me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, I didn’t think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back at elementary school, it was definitely there.\n\nTeachers were always moving my seat around because I talked too much, or I’d be getting up when I wasn’t supposed to, like without asking. And I remember blurting things out a lot—like they’d be halfway through a question and I’d already jump in with the answer because I thought I knew where they were going. At the time it was just treated like I was being disruptive or not listening, but now when I think back on it, it does sound like the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, a relationship, or an important opportunity because of these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or had a relationship end because of it, no. Not in that clean, obvious way where someone says, “This happened because you can’t sit still or you interrupt too much,” or something like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m still keeping up overall. I’m still working, still doing what I need to do, still present in my family’s life. But I can feel it costing me in smaller ways that add up. At work, if a meeting gets derailed because I jump in too much, or a mentoring conversation doesn’t go the way it should because I’m moving too fast or not letting the other person finish, people don’t necessarily punish me for it, but their patience changes. You can feel when people start managing around you a little.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar but more personal. If we have plans and I’m supposed to be engaged and the whole thing gets sidetracked, it chips away at trust. Not like my marriage is over or anything, but more like my wife has less confidence that things will go smoothly when I say they will. So I haven’t lost some major thing outright, but I do feel like I’m slowly spending down patience and trust, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity have significantly impacted your ability to succeed in your career or maintain healthy relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say yes, but I keep wanting to qualify it because I don’t feel like I’ve completely failed or fallen apart. I’m still keeping up overall. I’m working, I’m handling responsibilities, I’m present at home. So if “significantly impacted” means I can’t function, then no, it’s not that.\n\nBut if it means it’s starting to affect how people experience me and how much confidence they have in me, then yeah. At work, especially in meetings or mentoring conversations, if things don’t go the way they should because I move too fast or the conversation gets pulled off track, I can feel people’s patience thin out. It’s subtle, but it’s there. They might not say anything directly, but the room changes a little.\n\nAnd at home it’s more about trust. If we have family plans or my wife is counting on me to stay engaged and things get sidetracked, it feels like I’m chipping away at reliability. Like I’m not destroying the relationship, but I’m making it harder for her to trust that things will go smoothly when I say they will.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m succeeding on paper, but it’s costing me more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on something that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve definitely gotten wrapped up in things before, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as losing awareness of everything around me.\n\nWhen I hear it phrased that way, I think of someone who sits down and then suddenly hours have gone by and they forgot to eat or missed a bunch of important stuff. I don’t know that I have a clear example like that. I can get into something and be pretty absorbed, especially if it’s interesting or urgent, but I’m usually still kind of aware of the clock or people around me.\n\nSo… maybe mildly sometimes, but it’s not one of the things I’ve really noticed as a major pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so intensely focused on a task or activity that you tuned out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that intense way. I mean, I can get interested in something and spend longer on it than I meant to, but when you say “tuned out everything else,” I picture someone who doesn’t hear people talking to them or completely forgets what’s happening around them, and that doesn’t really sound like me.\n\nI’m usually at least partly aware of what’s going on. Like, I might be focused, but I’m not gone, if that makes sense. I don’t have a strong example where I was so locked in that the rest of the world disappeared. So I’d probably say no, or at least not something I’ve noticed as a real pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been noticeably harder to manage for about… a year and two months, maybe around that.\n\nIt really became more obvious after the conflict with my in-laws started taking up more space in our family life. Before that, I’m not saying everything was perfect, but it felt more manageable, like I could kind of compensate or keep things contained. Since then, it’s been harder to stay on top of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, it’s not just a few bad weeks or a short stressful patch. It’s been going on long enough that I can’t really write it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in the workplace, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it shows up in more than one place. I’m still functioning, like I’m not dropping everything or failing at work or anything that dramatic, but it’s starting to have a cost.\n\nAt home, it affects trust a bit. If family plans don’t go the way they should, or I don’t handle something the way I said I would, it’s not always seen as just a one-off anymore. It becomes this thing where people are bracing for me to be scattered or impatient or not fully steady with it, and that’s hard to admit.\n\nAnd at work, I can usually keep the bigger picture moving because that’s kind of my role, but in meetings or mentoring conversations, people can get frustrated with me. Not necessarily in a huge blowup way, more like I can feel their patience wearing thin when the conversation doesn’t stay as smooth or productive as it should.\n\nSo yes, home and work for sure. Socially maybe less obvious, but the main places are definitely family and work.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity as a young child, before the age of 7?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t have perfect memory from before age 7, obviously, but looking back, that pattern was there when I was a kid.\n\nI remember teachers moving my seat around a lot because I talked too much or I’d get up when I wasn’t supposed to. Not in a trying-to-be-bad way, at least not how I remember it. It was more like I’d just already be doing it before I thought about it. And I’d blurt things out before the teacher finished asking the question, which got me in trouble pretty regularly.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say it goes back pretty early. At the time people just treated it like I was energetic or disruptive, but looking back now, it does feel like the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant problems in your relationships with friends or family members because of your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly with family. I wouldn’t say my relationships are falling apart or anything like that, but it has definitely created strain.\n\nAt home, if there’s a plan I said I’d handle, or something needs to go a certain way and I’m not really on top of it, it can turn into more than just the one situation. It’s like people start to wonder whether they can count on me to follow through smoothly. And that hurts, because I do care, and I’m not trying to be careless or make things harder for anyone.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m still keeping up overall, but there’s a cost. The trust gets chipped at a little. Family members can get frustrated because they feel like they have to double-check me or brace for me to be impatient or scattered when plans don’t go the way they should.\n\nWith friends, I think it’s less obvious, or maybe I hide it better. But with family, yes, it has caused real tension. And at work too, not family obviously, but in meetings or mentoring conversations I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me when the conversation doesn’t go as smoothly as it should. So I’d say yes, it affects relationships, especially at home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity have led to you being unable to perform your job or daily responsibilities at the level you're expected to?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m unable to do my job or take care of daily responsibilities, exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. High school is a little clearer in my memory than when I was really young, but honestly when I look back, it feels like the same pattern had already been there for years.

Even as a kid, teachers were constantly moving my seat because I was talking too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt out answers before they were even done asking the question. By the time I was a teenager, it wasn’t like that just disappeared. I think people maybe expected me to have grown out of it more, so it stood out differently, but the basic behavior was familiar.

It wasn’t usually me trying to cause trouble. That’s the part I remember feeling misunderstood about. I’d just be talking, or already standing up, or already saying something, and then realize after the fact that I’d interrupted or done something I wasn’t supposed to do. So, yes, I’d say those behaviors were there in high school too, and they were a continuation of what teachers had been pointing out since I was younger.

I’d blurt things out before they even finished asking the question, like I thought I knew where they were going and I just had to answer right then. At work, I can keep the bigger responsibilities moving, but meetings or mentoring conversations don’t always go as smoothly as they should. Sometimes I can tell people are getting impatient with me, not because the whole thing has collapsed, but because it takes more effort than it should or I’m not as steady in the conversation as they need me to be.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. Family plans or things I said I’d handle can go sideways, and then it starts costing me trust. It’s not like I’m completely failing my responsibilities, but people start bracing themselves a little, like they’re expecting me to drop the ball or make things harder. That part bothers me a lot.\n\nSo… yes, it affects my level of performance, but more in this creeping way. I’m still getting by, but it takes a toll on trust at home and patience from people at work.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these same behaviors when you were a teenager, like in high school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. High school is a little clearer in my memory than when I was really young, but honestly when I look back, it feels like the same pattern had already been there for years.\n\nEven as a kid, teachers were constantly moving my seat because I was talking too much, or I’d get up without asking, or I’d blurt out answers before they were even done asking the question. By the time I was a teenager, it wasn’t like that just disappeared. I think people maybe expected me to have grown out of it more, so it stood out differently, but the basic behavior was familiar.\n\nIt wasn’t usually me trying to cause trouble. That’s the part I remember feeling misunderstood about. I’d just be talking, or already standing up, or already saying something, and then realize after the fact that I’d interrupted or done something I wasn’t supposed to do. So, yes, I’d say those behaviors were there in high school too, and they were a continuation of what teachers had been pointing out since I was younger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just something that started recently or only showed up when work got more demanding. It was there when I was a kid too.

I remember teachers moving my seat a lot because I’d talk too much, or I’d be getting up without asking, or I’d blurt something out before they even finished the question. At the time I don’t think I understood it as a pattern — it was more like, “Why am I always getting corrected?” But now, when I look back, it feels like the same kind of thing showing up at different ages.

As a teenager it was still there, and now as an adult it shows up in different settings, but it feels connected. The form changes a bit, but the underlying issue has been there for a long time.

Not every single second, but it happens a lot when I’m supposed to just sit still and be present. I’ll be with an employee and they’re explaining an issue, and before they’re done, I’m already finishing their thought or giving the solution I think they’re getting to. They’ll pause or get a little quiet, and then I realize I sort of steamrolled them.\n\nSo yeah, it’s less like I’m making huge impulsive decisions and more that my mouth gets ahead of me. I jump in before I’ve taken a second to think, “Should I actually speak right now, or should I let them finish?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity are causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is starting to cause problems, even if I’m not completely falling apart or anything. Like, I’m still getting through my responsibilities overall—I’m not missing everything or unable to function—but it feels like it’s costing me more than it used to.\n\nAt work, especially in meetings or when I’m mentoring someone, I can tell people are getting less patient with me. If I interrupt or move the conversation along too fast, or if I lose the thread and have to circle back, it changes the tone. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, but I’d probably describe it as significant in a gradual, wearing-people-down kind of way rather than like I can’t function at all.

At work, I’m still keeping up overall. I’m not losing everything or failing the main responsibilities. But there are these moments in meetings or mentoring conversations where things don’t go the way they should, and I can feel people’s patience thinning. Like I may get through the conversation, but it takes more effort from the other person than it should, or they leave feeling like I wasn’t as steady or easy to work with as they expected me to be.

And at home, it shows up with family plans or things I said I’d take care of. It’s not that I never do anything, but when something goes sideways, it starts to cost me trust. People start double-checking me or bracing for me to make it harder than it needs to be. That’s probably the part that feels the most socially painful — not one huge disaster, but this sense that people are getting less patient with me.

So yes, I’m functioning, but it’s not without consequences. I’m still getting by, but it’s affecting how much people trust me at home and how much patience people have with me at work.
